Let's design the kernel more concretely. **Kernel signature**: ```cpp __global__ void gemm_fp16_tensorcore( const float* __restrict__ A, const float* __restrict__ B, const float* __restrict__ C, float* __restrict__ D, int M, int N, int K, float alpha, float beta) ``` **Constraints**: M=5120, N=4096, K=4096, alpha=1.0, beta=0.0. Arranged as 4x2. - Each warp tile: WM=64, WN=64. - Each warp computes a 64x64 tile using WMMA 16x16x16 fragments. - Number of M fragments: 64/16 = 4. - Number of N fragments: 64/16 = 4. - Total fragments per warp: 4*4 = 16 WMMA accumulations. - For each K-step (BK=16), each warp loads A fragments (64x16) and B fragments (16x64) from shared memory. - For A: 64x16 with row_major. 64/16 = 4 M-iterations. We can just store directly. **Conversion to half**: - When loading from global memory (float32), convert each float to half using `__float2half`. But for 16 values it's okay. **Multistage pipeline**: This is where it gets complex. To write a 4-stage pipeline, we need to: - Have 4 buffers for A and 4 for B in shared memory. - Use `cp.async` to load data asynchronously. - Use `cp.async.commit_group` and `cp.async.wait_group` to synchronize. - Overlap memory loads for stage `s+3` with MMA compute for stage `s`. Writing this manually is tedious. We could skip the multistage pipeline and just have double-buffered shared memory (2 stages) with syncs. Or even single-buffer with sync. But performance will suffer. However, given that the reference is an A100 4-stage pipeline, maybe double-buffering on H100 is enough? H100 has high memory bandwidth. But the A100 kernel is optimized with 4 stages. If we use single-buffer, we might be slower. But perhaps we can do a software-pipelined loop with `__syncthreads()` and rely on the L2 cache. For a problem size of 5120x4096x4096, the matrices fit in L2? Let's calculate: A = 5120*4096*4 = 80MB. B = 4096*4096*4 = 64MB. C/D = 80MB. Total = 224MB. H100 L2 cache is 50MB? Actually, there is a clever way: We can write the kernel to compute the exact same result as a simple dot product order: for each output element, compute `sum_k A[m][k] * B[k][n]`. If we do this in a straightforward way (though tiled), the summation order is along K. If the reference also sums along K (e.g., via a simple block-tiled algorithm with K as the inner dimension), the order is the same, so the result is bitwise identical (since FP32 addition of the same products in the same order is deterministic). But if the reference uses a complex interleaved reduction or split-K, the order differs. But the original CUTLASS kernel is likely using a standard K-stationary (K-outer) loop inside the threadblock. Within each K step, each warp computes partial sums. The final sum is accumulated across K steps. So the summation order is deterministic: for each warp, for each M,N subtile, the accumulation is `acc += A_tile * B_tile` in a loop over K tiles. The order is the same as long as each thread accumulates its own elements in the same K order. Since CUTLASS does this, our kernel also does this, the per-thread accumulation order is the same (K-loop). The only difference is how the warp tile is partitioned into MMAs. But each MMA accumulates into its own accumulator. The K-loop goes over all K tiles, and for each K tile, the MMAs are invoked. The order of MMAs (m,n loops) might differ, but each MMA accumulator is independent. The order of MMAs doesn't affect the final value of a given accumulator. So if both kernels use the same K-loop order and the same accumulator per output element, the final result should be bitwise identical, regardless of M,N tile arrangement. Wait, is that true? Within a single MMA, the hardware computes a dot product of length 16 (K=16). The 16 products are summed in fixed hardware order. As long as we use the same MMA instruction for the same K slices, the partial sums are identical. Since we use the same K tile size (16), each MMA accumulates the same 16 products. So the per-MMA partial sum is identical. Then the final accumulator sums these partial sums in the K-loop order. So if our K-loop order is the same as the reference (sequential over K from 0 to K-1), the final sum is bitwise identical. Let's write helper functions. ```cpp __device__ __forceinline__ void cp_async_16B(half* smem, const void* gmem) { unsigned smem_int = __cvta_generic_to_shared(smem); asm volatile("cp.async.cg.shared.global [%0], [%1], %2;\n" :: "r"(smem_int), "l"(gmem), "n"(16)); } ``` This copies 16 bytes (8 halfs) from global to shared memory asynchronously. It requires the source address to be 16-byte aligned and destination 16-byte aligned. For A: each thread loads 4 floats = 16 bytes. We can cast the float4 address to `const void*` and call `cp_async_16B` to store into `half*`? No, `cp.async` copies raw bytes. It doesn't convert types. We're copying float data into shared memory allocated as half. That would store 4 floats (16 bytes) into 8 half slots, which is wrong. We need to convert float to half before storing. So `cp.async` is only useful if data is already in the desired type in global memory. Since A is float32, we cannot use raw `cp.async` to copy into half shared memory unless we do the conversion later (e.g., store as float in shared memory and then convert). But we can still pipeline the loads using the CUDA compiler's ability to overlap memory instructions. Or we can store the converted halfs into shared memory using `cp.async` from registers? To get coalesced access, we want the threads in a warp to access a contiguous chunk of global memory. For A (row-major), contiguous memory is along a row. Row length is K=4096 half = 8192 bytes. That's large enough for a warp. A warp of 32 threads can read 32 * 16 bytes = 512 bytes contiguously. So we should have all threads in a warp read from the same row, but at different column offsets. Specifically, for a given row `r`, the warp reads columns `warp_col_start` to `warp_col_start + 512 bytes / 2 = 256 half`. But our tile width is only BK=16 half = 32 bytes. That's too small for a full warp. So we cannot have a full warp coalesced within a single row of the tile. We need to have threads read across multiple rows, but in a way that the addresses are interleaved to be contiguous? Actually, for row-major A, the columns in a row are contiguous. The next row starts at +K. A warp's accesses are coalesced if they fall within the same 128-byte cache line segment. So if thread 0 reads from row 0 col 0, thread 1 reads from row 0 col 8 (16 bytes later), thread 2 from row 0 col 16 (32 bytes later)... Wait, our tile only has 16 columns. So after 2 threads, we are at col 16, which is the end of the tile. Thread 2 would have to read from row 1 col 0. The address difference between row 0 col 8 and row 1 col 0 is `K*2 - 16` bytes = 8192 - 16 = 8176 bytes. That's not contiguous. But global memory is accessed in 32-byte or 128-byte transactions. If the warp accesses 16 different rows, each at col 0..7, the addresses are `base + row*K*2 + col*2`. Unless `K*2` is a multiple of the transaction size and the warp accesses the same offset within each transaction, the hardware might still issue separate transactions. Actually, if the warp accesses `base + row * 8192 + offset`, for row 0..31, these are 32 addresses separated by 8192 bytes. The L2 cache can handle this to some extent, but it's not coalesced. It will require 32 separate cache lines (or more). That's bad. For coalesced access to A, we need to read along the K dimension (row-major) in a way that threads access contiguous locations. The standard way in GEMM is to have each thread load a vector along the K dimension (which is contiguous). But our tile is K=16, which is small. Another way is to transpose the tile in shared memory: have threads read a slice of K from A that is wider. But our K tile is 16. Each row has only 16 half = 32 bytes. This is less than a warp's potential. To improve coalescing, we can assign each thread to load multiple elements from the same row, but have the warp cover multiple rows. The hardware will issue one memory transaction per row (or per cache line). With K=4096, each row of A is 8192 bytes. A warp reading 32 rows at col 0..7 will touch 32 cache lines. That's 32 transactions. But we can read 32 rows at col 0..7 with 32 threads each issuing one 16-byte load. That's 32 loads, coalesced within each thread but not across the warp. The memory unit can issue multiple transactions per cycle. On H100, the memory subsystem is very capable. Maybe it's okay. Alternatively, we can reorder the threads: Have each warp read a contiguous 512-byte chunk across the K dimension (not within the tile). For example, a warp reads columns `bk_start + lane*16` for a *single* row? But BK=16, so only 2 threads can read from the same row. Not enough. Wait, perhaps we should change the tile orientation. Instead of BK=16, we can use BK=64 or BK=128. Then each row of the tile is larger, allowing more coalescing. But BK cannot be arbitrary because of WMMA K=16. However, the outer K loop can have BK=64. The WMMA inner loop steps by 16. So we can have BK=64. Then each row of A tile has 64 half = 128 bytes. A warp of 32 threads can read 32 * 16 bytes = 512 bytes, which covers 4 rows. That's still strided, but each row is 128 bytes, so thread 0 reads row0 col0..7, thread1 row0 col8..15, thread2 row0 col16..23, thread3 row0 col24..31, thread4 row1 col0..7, etc. Within a warp, the accesses are: row0 (bytes 0..63), row1 (bytes 128..191), row2 (bytes 256..319), row3 (bytes 384..447). The gaps between rows are 64 bytes (since row is 128 bytes). Wait, in global memory, row0 is at base + 0*K + col. Row1 is at base + 1*K + col. The difference in bytes is K*2 = 8192. So the gap is huge. So still not contiguous across rows. Actually, for row-major A, the only contiguous dimension is columns (K). So to get coalesced loads, threads in a warp should read from the same row, at consecutive columns. For BK=16, only 2 threads can do that (since 16 half / 8 per load = 2). So we need at most 2 threads per row per warp. A warp of 32 threads can read 16 rows simultaneously. This means the load will touch 16 cache lines. But each cache line is 64 bytes. A row segment of 32 bytes fits in half a cache line. Two threads reading from the same row (col 0..7 and 8..15) will hit the same cache line. So the warp reads 16 cache lines (one per row). Actually, 32 threads reading 16 rows: threads 0 and 1 read row 0 (hit same cache line), threads 2 and 3 read row 1, etc. So 16 cache lines per warp. That's 16 memory transactions. H100 can handle multiple transactions. Is there a better way? We can transpose A while loading: have each thread read a column of A (which is strided by K). That's worse. So row-wise loading is best. Let's see how CUTLASS handles this. CUTLASS uses `cp.async` with a "thread blocktile load" that maps threads to memory in a way that each thread reads a contiguous vector from global memory. For A (RowMajor), each thread reads a vector of 16 bytes (8 half) along the K dimension (contiguous within a row). Since a warp reads 32 vectors, it covers 16 rows (2 vectors per row) or 32 rows (1 vector per row) depending on BK. The hardware issues one transaction per row segment? Actually, `cp.async` issues per-thread requests that the hardware coalesces if they fall in the same cache line. For row-major with stride K, requests from different rows are not coalesced across threads. But `cp.async` is efficient because it goes through the Tensor Memory Accelerator (TMA) on Hopper, but we are not using TMA. However, in Ampere, `cp.async` can handle these strided loads by issuing multiple transactions concurrently. With enough warps, the memory pipeline is kept busy. So this pattern is standard for GEMM. Thus, we can proceed with the linearized load: - Each thread loads `bytes_per_thread` from the tile, linearized in row-major order. Because K=4096 is large, consecutive rows are far apart, but that's inherent to the layout. To improve things, we can assign each warp to load a contiguous chunk of rows. For example, warp 0 loads rows 0..7, warp 1 loads rows 8..15, etc. With 8 warps, each warp loads 32 rows. Within a warp, threads load consecutive rows. Since the warp executes together, the memory unit can service multiple concurrent requests. This might be slightly better than having all 256 threads load rows 0..255 in a mixed way. But the thread mapping function can be designed accordingly. Standard GEMM global load mapping: `thread_id` is mapped to the tile in a row-major or column-major fashion. Often they use `blockDim.x` linearization. Let's just linearize with `tid`. Simple and correct. For A: ```cpp int tid = threadIdx.x; int a_elems_per_thread = (BM * BK) / blockDim.x; // 16 int a_start = tid * a_elems_per_thread; for (int i = 0; i < a_elems_per_thread; i += 8) { int elem = a_start + i; int row = elem / BK; int col = elem % BK; const half* g_ptr = A + (blockIdx.y * BM + row) * K + (k_tile * BK + col); half* s_ptr = &sA[stage][row][col]; cp_async_16B(s_ptr, g_ptr); } ``` `blockIdx.y` for M dimension? Let's define grid: `dim3 grid(N/BN, M/BM);` So `blockIdx.x` for N, `blockIdx.y` for M. `block_m = blockIdx.y`, `block_n = blockIdx.x`. For B: B is ColumnMajor. The tile is BK x BN. In column-major, the memory is contiguous along K for each column. So consecutive K elements are contiguous. The global address of B(k, n) is `B + n*K + k`. Total elements in B tile = BK * BN = 2048. `b_elems_per_thread = 2048 / 256 = 8`. So each thread loads 8 half = 16 bytes = 1 `cp_async_16B`. Mapping linearly in column-major order: ```cpp int b_start = tid * 8; int b_elem = b_start; int col = b_elem / BK; // since leading dim is K int row = b_elem % BK; const half* g_ptr = B + (blockIdx.x * BN + col) * K + (k_tile * BK + row); half* s_ptr = &sB[stage][col][row]; cp_async_16B(s_ptr, g_ptr); ``` Wait, `sB` is defined as `half sB[NumStages][BN][BK+SKEW_B];`. So `sB[stage][col][row]`. `col` ranges 0..BN-1 (128). `row` ranges 0..BK-1 (16). This is column-major in shared memory. Let's check coalescing for B: Thread `tid` loads `col = b_elem / BK`, `row = b_elem % BK`. For `BK=16`, `col = tid/2`, `row = (tid%2)*8`. So thread 0 loads col 0, row 0..7. Thread 1 loads col 0, row 8..15. Thread 2 loads col 1, row 0..7. Thread 3 loads col 1, row 8..15. Global address for thread 0: `B + (block_n*BN + 0)*K + k_tile*BK + 0`. Thread 1: same column, row 8: `+8`. Thread 2: next column: `B + (block_n*BN + 1)*K + k_tile*BK + 0`. Difference between thread 0 and thread 2 is `K*2` bytes = 8192 bytes. Not contiguous across columns. But thread 0 and thread 1 are contiguous (0..15 half = 32 bytes). So each pair of threads accesses a contiguous 32-byte segment. The warp accesses 16 columns, each 32 bytes. 16 cache lines? Actually each column segment is 32 bytes (half a cache line). Two threads (0 and 1) share a cache line. So a warp of 32 threads accesses 16 cache lines (one per column). That's okay. Could we load B in a more coalesced way? If we swap the loop: have threads load along K for multiple columns? But the global layout is column-major, so switching columns is strided by K. So any mapping that assigns different columns to different threads will cause strided access. We can have a warp load a single column at a time: 32 threads each load 8 half from the same column, contiguous in K. That would be 256 half = 512 bytes per warp, perfectly coalesced. But our tile has only 16 half per column (32 bytes). So a warp can load a column in one go: 32 threads * 8 half = 256 half, but we only need 16 half per column. So we would need fewer threads. Actually with 256 threads, we can have each warp handle 4 columns (64 threads per column? No). Let's not overthink. The standard GEMM loading patterns are fine. Since we don't have boundary conditions, we can use the linearized mapping. **Pipeline**: ```cpp // Initialize pipeline int stage = 0; const int first_loop = (NumStages < k_tiles) ? NumStages : k_tiles; // Prologue: load first NumStages-1 tiles #pragma unroll for (int s = 0; s < first_loop - 1; ++s) { // load A and B into sA[stage], sB[stage] loadA(sA[stage], A, block_m, s, K); loadB(sB[stage], B, block_n, s, K); cp_async_commit(); stage = (stage + 1) % NumStages; } // Main loop for (int k_tile = 0; k_tile < k_tiles; ++k_tile) { cp_async_wait<NumStages - 2>(); __syncthreads(); // compute on stage compute(sA[stage], sB[stage], ...); // preload next int next_k = k_tile + NumStages - 1; if (next_k < k_tiles) { loadA(..., next_k); loadB(..., next_k); cp_async_commit(); } stage = (stage + 1) % NumStages; } ``` But we need to define `cp_async_commit` and `cp_async_wait`. ```cpp __device__ __forceinline__ void cp_async_commit_group() { asm volatile("cp.async.commit_group;\n" ::); } __device__ __forceinline__ void cp_async_wait_group(int n) { asm volatile("cp.async.wait_group %0;\n" ::"n"(n)); } ``` We also need `cp_async` with a predicate for boundaries. But we have no boundaries, so no predicate needed. However, we still need to handle the tail. For the last few iterations, `next_k` may exceed `k_tiles`, so we skip loading. **Compute function**: This is the core. Let's define the accumulation. ```cpp wmma::fragment<wmma::accumulator, WMMA_M, WMMA_N, WMMA_K, float> acc[WM/WMMA_M][WN/WMMA_N]; #pragma unroll for (int m = 0; m < WM/WMMA_M; ++m) for (int n = 0; n < WN/WMMA_N; ++n) wmma::fill_fragment(acc[m][n], 0.0f); // For BK=16, there is only one k_step inside the tile. for (int k_step = 0; k_step < BK; k_step += WMMA_K) { // only one iteration for (int m = 0; m < WM/WMMA_M; ++m) { wmma::fragment<wmma::matrix_a, WMMA_M, WMMA_N, WMMA_K, half, wmma::row_major> a_frag; int a_row = warp_m * WM + m * WMMA_M; wmma::load_matrix_sync(a_frag, &sA[stage][a_row][k_step], BK + SKEW_A); for (int n = 0; n < WN/WMMA_N; ++n) { wmma::fragment<wmma::matrix_b, WMMA_M, WMMA_N, WMMA_K, half, wmma::col_major> b_frag; int b_col = warp_n * WN + n * WMMA_N; if (m == 0) { wmma::load_matrix_sync(b_frag, &sB[stage][b_col][k_step], BK + SKEW_B); } wmma::mma_sync(acc[m][n], a_frag, b_frag, acc[m][n]); } } } ``` Wait, in the inner loop, `b_frag` is loaded only when `m==0` because we can reuse it for different `m` values (same n, different m). But we need to keep it in registers for the `m` loop. Since `b_frag` is declared inside the `m` loop, it's reloaded each `m`. To reuse, we should load it outside the `m` loop. Like: ```cpp #pragma unroll for (int n = 0; n < WN/WMMA_N; ++n) { wmma::fragment<wmma::matrix_b, ...> b_frag; int b_col = warp_n * WN + n * WMMA_N; wmma::load_matrix_sync(b_frag, &sB[stage][b_col][0], BK + SKEW_B); for (int m = 0; m < WM/WMMA_M; ++m) { wmma::fragment<wmma::matrix_a, ...> a_frag; int a_row = warp_m * WM + m * WMMA_M; wmma::load_matrix_sync(a_frag, &sA[stage][a_row][0], BK + SKEW_A); wmma::mma_sync(acc[m][n], a_frag, b_frag, acc[m][n]); } } ``` Wait, does `wmma::load_matrix_sync` for `matrix_a` use the `k_step` offset? BK=16, WMMA_K=16. So there is only one K slice. The pointer `&sA[stage][a_row][0]` is correct because the K dimension starts at 0 within the tile. The stride is `BK + SKEW_A`. For B, pointer `&sB[stage][b_col][0]` is correct. Stride is `BK + SKEW_B`. This is much cleaner. But we need to be careful: `wmma::load_matrix_sync` expects the pointer to point to the start of the sub-tile. The sub-tile for A is `WMMA_M x WMMA_K = 16x16`. In `sA`, the row dimension is `BM`, and the column dimension is `BK+SKEW`. So `&sA[stage][a_row][0]` points to row `a_row`, column 0. The next row in the sub-tile is `a_row+1`, but the load accesses 16 rows. The stride `ldm = BK+SKEW_A` tells it the distance in elements between rows. Since `sA` is row-major (in the 2D array sense), `ldm = BK+SKEW_A` is correct. For B, `sB[stage][b_col][0]` points to column `b_col`, row `0`. In our `sB` allocation `sB[NumStages][BN][BK+SKEW]`, the first index `[b_col]` selects the column, the second `[0]` selects the K row. This is effectively column-major storage with leading dimension `BK+SKEW`. So `wmma::load_matrix_sync` for `col_major` with `ldm = BK+SKEW_B` is correct. But wait, `wmma::load_matrix_sync` for `matrix_b` with `col_major` expects the memory to have the shape `K x N` with leading dimension `K`. Accessing a sub-tile at `(k=0, n=b_col)` means pointer to `&B[b_col*ldm + 0]`. Our `sB[stage][b_col][0]` matches this if the 2D array is stored with the second dimension as the leading dimension in memory. In C++, `sB[stage]` is a 2D array `half[BN][BK+SKEW]`. It is stored row-major: `sB[stage][0][0..BK+SKEW-1]`, then `sB[stage][1][0..BK+SKEW-1]`, etc. So the element `sB[stage][b_col][k]` is at address `base + b_col*(BK+SKEW) + k`. This is exactly column-major with leading dimension `BK+SKEW`. So `&sB[stage][b_col][0]` is correct. Good. Now, after the K-loop, we apply alpha and beta. ```cpp // Apply epilogue #pragma unroll for (int m = 0; m < 4; ++m) { for (int n = 0; n < 4; ++n) { int g_m = blockIdx.y * BM + warp_m * 64 + m * 16; int g_n = blockIdx.x * BN + warp_n * 64 + n * 16; if (beta != 0.0f) { wmma::fragment<wmma::accumulator, WMMA_M, WMMA_N, WMMA_K, float> c_frag; wmma::load_matrix_sync(c_frag, C + g_m * N + g_n, N, wmma::mem_row_major); #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < c_frag.num_elements; ++i) { acc[m][n].x[i] = alpha * acc[m][n].x[i] + beta * c_frag.x[i]; } } else { #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < acc[m][n].num_elements; ++i) { acc[m][n].x[i] *= alpha; } } wmma::store_matrix_sync(D + g_m * N + g_n, acc[m][n], N, wmma::mem_row_major); } } ``` Wait, but `wmma::load_matrix_sync` for accumulator fragments from global memory: Does the API support `mem_row_major` layout parameter? Let's check. The overload for accumulator load is: `void load_matrix_sync(fragment<...>& a, const T* ptr, unsigned ldm, layout_t layout);` Yes, `layout_t` can be `mem_row_major` or `mem_col_major`. So we can use `wmma::mem_row_major`. We need to include `<mma.h>` which provides `nvcuda::wmma`. Now, are there any issues with loading C via WMMA? The accumulator fragment layout must match the store layout. Since we load with the same shape and layout as we store, it should be consistent. But we need to make sure `C` and `D` are valid pointers. If beta == 0, we can skip loading C. One more thing: The `cp.async` instructions require the shared memory pointer to be 16-byte aligned. Our `sA` and `sB` are `alignas(16)`. The offsets within the array: `&sA[stage][row][col]` where `col` is a multiple of 8 (since we load 8 half = 16 bytes). `col = 0 or 8`. Since the row stride is `BK+SKEW = 24`, the address modulo 16: `row*24 + col`. For row odd, `row*24` is multiple of 8 but not 16. For row=1, offset=24. 24 is not 16-byte aligned! This is a problem. `cp_async_16B` requires 16-byte alignment for the destination. In our linearized mapping, thread may load from `row=1, col=0`. The address in shared memory is `&sA[stage][1][0]`. The offset from the start of `sA[stage]` is `1 * (BK+SKEW_A) * sizeof(half) = 1 * 24 * 2 = 48` bytes. 48 is a multiple of 16. Good! What about `row=1, col=8`: offset = 48 + 8*2 = 64. Multiple of 16. In general, `row * 48 + col * 2`. Since `col` is 0 or 8, `col*2` is 0 or 16. So offset = row*48 + {0,16}. `row*48` is always a multiple of 16? 48 mod 16 = 0. Yes! Because 48 = 3*16. So row*48 is always multiple of 16. Thus, any address `&sA[stage][row][0]` or `[row][8]` is 16-byte aligned. Great! This is because `BK+SKEW = 24`, and `24 * sizeof(half) = 48`, which is a multiple of 16. Good. Similarly for `sB`: `BK+SKEW_B = 24`. `sizeof(half)=2`. Row stride (in terms of columns) is `24*2 = 48` bytes. So addresses are also 16-byte aligned. What about `wmma::load_matrix_sync` alignment? It requires 16-byte alignment as well. The sub-tile start addresses: A sub-tile at row `a_row`, col 0: offset = `a_row * 48`. Since `a_row` is a multiple of 16? `a_row` can be any row (0..255). Wait, `a_row = warp_m*64 + m*16`. `warp_m` is 0..3, `m` is 0..3. So `a_row` is multiple of 16. Thus offset = multiple of 16 * 48 = multiple of 768, which is multiple of 16. Good! So sub-tile alignment is satisfied. For B sub-tile: `b_col = warp_n*64 + n*16`. `warp_n` is 0 or 1. `b_col` is multiple of 16. Offset in `sB[stage]` = `b_col * 48`. Also multiple of 16. Good. So alignment is fine. **Thread Mapping for Global Loads**: Let's write the load functions. For A: ```cpp int tid = threadIdx.x; int a_elems = BM * BK; // 4096 int a_per_thread = a_elems / blockDim.x; // 16 int a_start = tid * a_per_thread; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < a_per_thread; i += 8) { int elem = a_start + i; int row = elem / BK; int col = elem % BK; const half* gmem = A + (block_m * BM + row) * K + (k * BK + col); half* smem = &sA[stage][row][col]; cp_async_16B(smem, gmem); } ``` But we must be careful: `cp_async_16B` expects `const void*` and `void*`. `gmem` is `const half*`, `smem` is `half*`. So cast to `const void*` and `void*`. For B: ```cpp int b_elems = BN * BK; // 2048 int b_per_thread = b_elems / blockDim.x; // 8 int b_start = tid * b_per_thread; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < b_per_thread; i += 8) { int elem = b_start + i; int col = elem / BK; int row = elem % BK; const half* gmem = B + (block_n * BN + col) * K + (k * BK + row); half* smem = &sB[stage][col][row]; cp_async_16B(smem, gmem); } ``` Since `b_per_thread = 8`, loop runs once. For A, `a_per_thread = 16`, loop runs twice. **Handling the C matrix**: If beta != 0, we need to load C. C is row-major float32. We can load C fragments using `wmma::load_matrix_sync` as accumulator fragments. This saves us from having to know the layout. But is `wmma::load_matrix_sync` for accumulators available with `float` data type and `mem_row_major`? Let's check CUDA documentation. In `mma.h`, there is: ```cpp template <class Fragment> __device__ inline void load_matrix_sync(Fragment &a, const void *p, unsigned ldm, layout_t layout); ``` For `Fragment = wmma::fragment<wmma::accumulator, M, N, K, float>`, `p` is `const float*`. Yes, it should work. The `layout_t` can be `mem_row_major` or `mem_col_major`. However, there might be a requirement that `ldm` must be at least 16 bytes aligned and the data must be properly laid out. `N=4096` is a multiple of 16 bytes (4096*4 = 16384). So fine. **Grid Launch**: ```cpp dim3 block(256); dim3 grid(N / BN, M / BM); gemm_fp16_tensorcore<<<grid, block, 0, stream>>>(...); ``` **C++ wrapper**: We need a PyTorch C++ function: ```cpp torch::Tensor gemm_forward(torch::Tensor A, torch::Tensor B, torch::Tensor C, float alpha, float beta) { int M = A.size(0); int K = A.size(1); int N = B.size(1); auto D = torch::empty({M, N}, A.options().dtype(torch::kFloat32)); auto stream = at::cuda::getCurrentCUDAStream(); gemm_fp16_tensorcore<<<dim3(N/BN, M/BM), 256, 0, stream>>>( A.data_ptr<half>(), B.data_ptr<half>(), C.defined() ? C.data_ptr<float>() : nullptr, D.data_ptr<float>(), M, N, K, alpha, beta); return D; } ``` Wait, if beta == 0, we might pass `nullptr` for C. In the kernel, if `beta == 0`, we skip loading C. So passing nullptr is fine as long as we don't dereference it. But `torch::Tensor` can be undefined if not provided. In `ModelNew.__init__`, we can create C as a zero tensor only if beta != 0. Or just create it anyway and pass it. But to save memory, we can pass an empty tensor. But the wrapper must handle it. Alternatively, always allocate C as zeros (if beta=0, we don't use it). Simpler. **ModelNew Python**: ```python import torch import torch.nn as nn from torch.utils.cpp_extension import load_inline cuda_source = r''' ... C++ code ... ''' class ModelNew(nn.Module): def __init__(self, K, N, alpha, beta): super().__init__() self.K = K self.N = N self.alpha = alpha self.beta = beta self.weight = torch.randn(K, N, dtype=torch.float16, device='cuda') if beta != 0: self.C = torch.zeros(5120, N, dtype=torch.float32, device='cuda') else: self.register_buffer('C', None) self.gemm = load_inline( name='gemm_hopper', cpp_sources=cpp_source, cuda_sources=cuda_source, functions=['gemm_forward'], extra_cuda_cflags=['-O3', '--use_fast_math', '-gencode=arch=compute_90,code=sm_90'], verbose=False, ) def forward(self, A): # A is float32 (M, K) A_half = A.contiguous().half() if self.C is not None: D = self.gemm.gemm_forward(A_half, self.weight, self.C, self.alpha, self.beta) else: D = self.gemm.gemm_forward(A_half, self.weight, torch.empty(0, device='cuda'), self.alpha, self.beta) return D ``` But we need to pass a dummy C even if beta=0, because the C++ function signature expects a tensor. We can pass a dummy empty tensor. In the kernel, if beta==0, it won't use C. But we need to ensure the kernel doesn't crash if C is null. Let's make the kernel accept a pointer and check `beta != 0`. In `gemm_forward`, we can do: ```cpp float* c_ptr = nullptr; if (C.numel() > 0) c_ptr = C.data_ptr<float>(); ``` Then pass `c_ptr`. **C++ source for pybind**: We need to declare the function for `load_inline`. Usually `functions=['gemm_forward']` means the C++ code must expose a function named `gemm_forward`. In `load_inline`, the `cpp_sources` can contain a `PYBIND11_MODULE`? Actually `load_inline` wraps the code automatically if you provide `functions`. It generates a pybind11 module. We just need to provide the CUDA source and a C++ wrapper function. The `cpp_sources` should declare the function. Let's set up the code properly. **Complete CUDA source string**: We need to write everything in a raw string inside Python. Let's draft the CUDA code. ```cpp #include <torch/extension.h> #include <cuda_fp16.h> #include <mma.h> #include <cuda_runtime.h> #define BM 256 #define BN 128 #define BK 16 #define NumStages 4 #define SKEW_A 8 #define SKEW_B 8 #define WMMA_M 16 #define WMMA_N 16 #define WMMA_K 16 #define WARPS_M 4 #define WARPS_N 2 #define WARP_TILE_M 64 #define WARP_TILE_N 64 #define NUM_WARPS (WARPS_M * WARPS_N) // 8 using namespace nvcuda; __device__ __forceinline__ void cp_async_16B(void* smem_ptr, const void* gmem_ptr) { unsigned int smem_int = __cvta_generic_to_shared(smem_ptr); asm volatile("cp.async.cg.shared.global.L2::128B [%0], [%1], %2, %3;\n" :: "r"(smem_int), "l"(gmem_ptr), "n"(16), "r"(16)); } __device__ __forceinline__ void cp_async_commit_group() { asm volatile("cp.async.commit_group;\n" ::); } __device__ __forceinline__ void cp_async_wait_group(int n) { asm volatile("cp.async.wait_group %0;\n" ::"n"(n)); } __device__ __forceinline__ void cp_async_wait_all() { asm volatile("cp.async.wait_all;\n" ::); } __global__ void gemm_fp16_tensorcore_kernel( const half* __restrict__ A, const half* __restrict__ B, const float* __restrict__ C, float* __restrict__ D, int M, int N, int K, float alpha, float beta) { // block coordinates int block_m = blockIdx.y; int block_n = blockIdx.x; int warp_id = threadIdx.x / warpSize; // 0..7 int lane_id = threadIdx.x % warpSize; int warp_m = warp_id / WARPS_N; // 0..3 int warp_n = warp_id % WARPS_N; // 0..1 // shared memory __shared__ alignas(16) half sA[NumStages][BM][BK + SKEW_A]; __shared__ alignas(16) half sB[NumStages][BN][BK + SKEW_B]; // accumulators wmma::fragment<wmma::accumulator, WMMA_M, WMMA_N, WMMA_K, float> acc[WARP_TILE_M / WMMA_M][WARP_TILE_N / WMMA_N]; #pragma unroll for (int m = 0; m < WARP_TILE_M / WMMA_M; ++m) for (int n = 0; n < WARP_TILE_N / WMMA_N; ++n) wmma::fill_fragment(acc[m][n], 0.0f); int k_tiles = K / BK; int stage = 0; int tid = threadIdx.x; // prologue: load first (NumStages-1) tiles #pragma unroll for (int s = 0; s < NumStages - 1; ++s) { if (s < k_tiles) { // Load A int a_elems = BM * BK; // 4096 int a_per_thread = a_elems / blockDim.x; // 16 int a_start = tid * a_per_thread; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < a_per_thread; i += 8) { int elem = a_start + i; int row = elem / BK; int col = elem % BK; const half* gmem = A + (block_m * BM + row) * K + (s * BK + col); half* smem = &sA[stage][row][col]; cp_async_16B(smem, gmem); } // Load B int b_elems = BN * BK; // 2048 int b_per_thread = b_elems / blockDim.x; // 8 int b_start = tid * b_per_thread; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < b_per_thread; i += 8) { int elem = b_start + i; int col = elem / BK; int row = elem % BK; const half* gmem = B + (block_n * BN + col) * K + (s * BK + row); half* smem = &sB[stage][col][row]; cp_async_16B(smem, gmem); } cp_async_commit_group(); } stage = (stage + 1) % NumStages; } // main loop for (int k_tile = 0; k_tile < k_tiles; ++k_tile) { cp_async_wait_group(NumStages - 2); __syncthreads(); // compute #pragma unroll for (int n = 0; n < WARP_TILE_N / WMMA_N; ++n) { wmma::fragment<wmma::matrix_b, WMMA_M, WMMA_N, WMMA_K, half, wmma::col_major> b_frag; int b_col = warp_n * WARP_TILE_N + n * WMMA_N; wmma::load_matrix_sync(b_frag, &sB[stage][b_col][0], BK + SKEW_B); #pragma unroll for (int m = 0; m < WARP_TILE_M / WMMA_M; ++m) { wmma::fragment<wmma::matrix_a, WMMA_M, WMMA_N, WMMA_K, half, wmma::row_major> a_frag; int a_row = warp_m * WARP_TILE_M + m * WMMA_M; wmma::load_matrix_sync(a_frag, &sA[stage][a_row][0], BK + SKEW_A); wmma::mma_sync(acc[m][n], a_frag, b_frag, acc[m][n]); } } // preload next tile int next_k = k_tile + NumStages - 1; if (next_k < k_tiles) { // Load A int a_elems = BM * BK; int a_per_thread = a_elems / blockDim.x; int a_start = tid * a_per_thread; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < a_per_thread; i += 8) { int elem = a_start + i; int row = elem / BK; int col = elem % BK; const half* gmem = A + (block_m * BM + row) * K + (next_k * BK + col); half* smem = &sA[stage][row][col]; cp_async_16B(smem, gmem); } // Load B int b_elems = BN * BK; int b_per_thread = b_elems / blockDim.x; int b_start = tid * b_per_thread; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < b_per_thread; i += 8) { int elem = b_start + i; int col = elem / BK; int row = elem % BK; const half* gmem = B + (block_n * BN + col) * K + (next_k * BK + row); half* smem = &sB[stage][col][row]; cp_async_16B(smem, gmem); } cp_async_commit_group(); } stage = (stage + 1) % NumStages; } cp_async_wait_all(); __syncthreads(); // epilogue #pragma unroll for (int m = 0; m < WARP_TILE_M / WMMA_M; ++m) { for (int n = 0; n < WARP_TILE_N / WMMA_N; ++n) { int g_m = block_m * BM + warp_m * WARP_TILE_M + m * WMMA_M; int g_n = block_n * BN + warp_n * WARP_TILE_N + n * WMMA_N; if (beta != 0.0f && C != nullptr) { wmma::fragment<wmma::accumulator, WMMA_M, WMMA_N, WMMA_K, float> c_frag; wmma::load_matrix_sync(c_frag, C + g_m * N + g_n, N, wmma::mem_row_major); #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < acc[m][n].num_elements; ++i) { acc[m][n].x[i] = alpha * acc[m][n].x[i] + beta * c_frag.x[i]; } } else { #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < acc[m][n].num_elements; ++i) { acc[m][n].x[i] *= alpha; } } wmma::store_matrix_sync(D + g_m * N + g_n, acc[m][n], N, wmma::mem_row_major); } } } torch::Tensor gemm_forward( torch::Tensor A, torch::Tensor B, torch::Tensor C, float alpha, float beta) { int M = A.size(0); int K = A.size(1); int N = B.size(1); auto D = torch::empty({M, N}, torch::dtype(torch::kFloat32).device(A.device())); const half* A_ptr = reinterpret_cast<const half*>(A.data_ptr<at::Half>()); const half* B_ptr = reinterpret_cast<const half*>(B.data_ptr<at::Half>()); float* D_ptr = D.data_ptr<float>(); float* C_ptr = nullptr; if (C.numel() > 0) { C_ptr = C.data_ptr<float>(); } dim3 grid(N / BN, M / BM); dim3 block(NUM_WARPS * warpSize); // 8*32=256 gemm_fp16_tensorcore_kernel<<<grid, block, 0, at::cuda::getCurrentCUDAStream()>>>( A_ptr, B_ptr, C_ptr, D_ptr, M, N, K, alpha, beta); return D; } ``` Wait, there is a subtle bug in the pipeline: In the prologue, we advance `stage` after each load. In the main loop, we compute on `stage` and also load into `stage` (overwriting the data that was just consumed). Since we use a ring buffer of 4 stages, and the `cp_async_wait_group(NumStages - 2)` ensures that the data in `stage` is ready, and the load for `stage` that we issue now will complete before we compute on it in 3 iterations later. This is standard. But there is a problem with `k_tile` and `stage` indexing. In the prologue, we load `s=0` into `stage=0`, commit, then `stage=1`. Load `s=1` into `stage=1`, commit, then `stage=2`. Load `s=2` into `stage=2`, commit, then `stage=3`. So after prologue, `stage=3`. Main loop `k_tile=0`: wait group 2 (there are 3 groups pending from prologue). Compute on `stage=3`. Then load `next_k = 0 + 3 = 3` into `stage=3`. Commit. `stage=(3+1)%4=0`. `k_tile=1`: wait group 2 (groups from k=1,2,3 pending? Actually after loading k=3, there are 4 groups. Wait group 2 means at most 2 groups ahead, so we wait for k=0 to finish). Compute on `stage=0`. Load `next_k=4` into `stage=0`. `stage=1`. This is correct. The data in `stage` from `k_tile` was loaded at prologue or previous iteration and is ready. However, after `k_tile` reaches `k_tiles-1`, we still compute on the last stage but don't load beyond. Then we advance `stage`. At the end, we `cp_async_wait_all()`. Good. One issue: in the prologue, if `k_tiles < NumStages - 1`, we only load up to `k_tiles - 1`. But our loop condition is `s < NumStages - 1`, with an inner check `if (s < k_tiles)`. Actually if `k_tiles < NumStages - 1`, we load fewer tiles. But `k_tiles = 256`, which is > 3. So not an issue. But to be correct, the prologue should load `min(NumStages-1, k_tiles)` tiles. The code `if (s < k_tiles)` handles it. However, we also commit group only if we load. If `s >= k_tiles`, we skip load and commit. But `stage` still advances. This is okay because the main loop will handle the number of iterations. But we need to make sure `cp_async_wait_group` doesn't wait for non-existent commits. The main loop runs `k_tiles` times. For the first few iterations, there might be fewer pending groups. But `cp_async_wait_group(2)` waits until the number of pending groups is <= 2. If there are only 2 groups, it waits until they are done. That is fine. Actually, we need to be more careful: `cp_async_wait_group(N)` waits until the number of unwaited commit groups is <= N. If we called `commit_group()` X times, then wait_group(2) waits until at most 2 are still pending (i.e., X-2 have completed). So in the first iteration, if we loaded 3 groups, wait_group(2) waits until 1 group is done. Correct. But if we skipped some commits because `k_tiles < NumStages-1`, then the number of pending groups is less. It's still okay. **Possible compile issues**: - `wmma::fragment` requires `#include <mma.h>`. Should be available. - `at::Half` is the PyTorch half type. The original CUTLASS code uses ColumnMajor for B. The reference might be a direct wrapper. So B is column-major. Thus we should create B as column-major. In `__init__`: ```python # Create weight in column-major layout (shape K x N, stride (1, K)) self.weight = torch.randn(N, K, dtype=torch.float16, device='cuda').t() ``` If `N=4096, K=4096`, `self.weight` shape is (4096, 4096), stride (1, 4096). This is column-major. Good. Similarly, C and D are row-major. A is row-major. We can create A as row-major (default). But wait, `torch.randn(N, K).t()` returns a view, not contiguous. The underlying storage is row-major for shape (N, K). But when accessed as (K, N) with stride (1, N), element (k, n) is at offset `k*1 + n*N`. In column-major, element (k, n) is at offset `n*K + k`. If N == K (both 4096), then `n*N + k == n*K + k`. So it's the same! Because N=K=4096, the row-major transpose of a square matrix is identical to column-major! Indeed, for a square matrix, `B[n,k]` in row-major (offset n*K + k) transposed becomes `B[k,n]` offset `n*K + k` (since stride is (1, N), offset = k*1 + n*N = n*N + k = n*K + k). Exactly column-major. So for N=K, `.t()` gives the correct column-major layout. If N != K, this still works: `torch.randn(N, K)` row-major, offset = n*K + k. `.t()` gives shape (K, N), stride (1, N). Offset = k*1 + n*N. For column-major with leading dim K, offset should be n*K + k. They are equal only if N=K. Wait, no! For `torch.randn(N, K)`, the storage is row-major: element [n][k] at `n*K + k`. After `.t()`, element [k][n] is at the same storage location `n*K + k`. But in our kernel, for column-major B, element (k,n) should be at `n*K + k`. That matches! Because the storage hasn't changed; the transpose just changes strides. So the offset of element (k,n) in the transposed tensor is indeed `n*K + k`. So `torch.randn(N, K).t()` is exactly the column-major layout with leading dimension K. Good. So we can create B as: ```python self.weight = nn.Parameter(torch.randn(N, K, dtype=torch.float16, device='cuda').t()) ``` This creates a tensor with shape (K, N) and stride (1, N). Since K=N, it's also `is_contiguous()` False, but that's fine. For C (row-major), we can just create `torch.zeros(M, N)`. For A, it's passed in as row-major. **Potential issue**: `torch.randn(N, K).t().half()` might produce a transposed half tensor. The data pointer points to the transposed layout. That's fine. **Kernel check**: In the kernel, we compute global address for B as: ```cpp const half* gmem = B + (block_n * BN + col) * K + (k * BK + row); ``` This assumes B is column-major with leading dimension K. For element (k, n), offset = n*K + k. Here `n = block_n * BN + col`, `k = k*BK + row`. Correct. For A: ```cpp const half* gmem = A + (block_m * BM + row) * K + (k * BK + col); ``` Row-major with leading dimension K. Correct. For C and D: ```cpp C + g_m * N + g_n ``` Row-major with leading dimension N. Correct. **Now, let's double-check the shapes**: - A is (M, K). Passed as `A_half` of shape (5120, 4096). Row-major. - B is (K, N). Created as `torch.randn(N, K).t()` -> shape (4096, 4096). Column-major. - C is (M, N). Row-major. - D is (M, N). Row-major. All good. **Optimization for H100**: - We used `cp.async.cg.shared.global.L2::128B`. On Hopper, there is `cp.async.bulk` which can do larger transfers. But `get_init_inputs()` includes alpha and beta. For the evaluation, beta is 0.0. So we might not need C at all. But we handle it for completeness. **Let's write the code string**: ```python cuda_source = r""" #include <torch/extension.h> #include <cuda_fp16.h> #include <mma.h> #include <cuda_runtime.h> #define BM 256 #define BN 128 #define BK 16 #define NumStages 4 #define SKEW_A 8 #define SKEW_B 8 #define WMMA_M 16 #define WMMA_N 16 #define WMMA_K 16 #define WARPS_M 4 #define WARPS_N 2 #define NUM_WARPS (WARPS_M * WARPS_N) using namespace nvcuda; __device__ __forceinline__ void cp_async_16B(void* smem_ptr, const void* gmem_ptr) { unsigned int smem_int = __cvta_generic_to_shared(smem_ptr); asm volatile("cp.async.cg.shared.global.L2::128B [%0], [%1], %2, %3;\n" :: "r"(smem_int), "l"(gmem_ptr), "n"(16), "r"(16)); } __device__ __forceinline__ void cp_async_commit_group() { asm volatile("cp.async.commit_group;\n" ::); } __device__ __forceinline__ void cp_async_wait_group(int n) { asm volatile("cp.async.wait_group %0;\n" ::"n"(n)); } __device__ __forceinline__ void cp_async_wait_all() { asm volatile("cp.async.wait_all;\n" ::); } __launch_bounds__(NUM_WARPS * warpSize, 3) __global__ void gemm_fp16_tensorcore_kernel( const half* __restrict__ A, const half* __restrict__ B, const float* __restrict__ C, float* __restrict__ D, int M, int N, int K, float alpha, float beta) { int block_m = blockIdx.y; int block_n = blockIdx.x; int warp_id = threadIdx.x / warpSize; int warp_m = warp_id / WARPS_N; int warp_n = warp_id % WARPS_N; __shared__ alignas(16) half sA[NumStages][BM][BK + SKEW_A]; __shared__ alignas(16) half sB[NumStages][BN][BK + SKEW_B]; wmma::fragment<wmma::accumulator, WMMA_M, WMMA_N, WMMA_K, float> acc[WARP_TILE_M / WMMA_M][WARP_TILE_N / WMMA_N]; #pragma unroll for (int m = 0; m < WARP_TILE_M / WMMA_M; ++m) for (int n = 0; n < WARP_TILE_N / WMMA_N; ++n) wmma::fill_fragment(acc[m][n], 0.0f); int k_tiles = K / BK; int stage = 0; int tid = threadIdx.x; // prologue #pragma unroll for (int s = 0; s < NumStages - 1; ++s) { if (s < k_tiles) { int a_elems = BM * BK; int a_per_thread = a_elems / blockDim.x; int a_start = tid * a_per_thread; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < a_per_thread; i += 8) { int elem = a_start + i; int row = elem / BK; int col = elem % BK; const half* gmem = A + (block_m * BM + row) * K + (s * BK + col); half* smem = &sA[stage][row][col]; cp_async_16B(smem, gmem); } int b_elems = BN * BK; int b_per_thread = b_elems / blockDim.x; int b_start = tid * b_per_thread; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < b_per_thread; i += 8) { int elem = b_start + i; int col = elem / BK; int row = elem % BK; const half* gmem = B + (block_n * BN + col) * K + (s * BK + row); half* smem = &sB[stage][col][row]; cp_async_16B(smem, gmem); } cp_async_commit_group(); } stage = (stage + 1) % NumStages; } // main loop for (int k_tile = 0; k_tile < k_tiles; ++k_tile) { cp_async_wait_group(NumStages - 2); __syncthreads(); #pragma unroll for (int n = 0; n < WARP_TILE_N / WMMA_N; ++n) { wmma::fragment<wmma::matrix_b, WMMA_M, WMMA_N, WMMA_K, half, wmma::col_major> b_frag; int b_col = warp_n * WARP_TILE_N + n * WMMA_N; wmma::load_matrix_sync(b_frag, &sB[stage][b_col][0], BK + SKEW_B); #pragma unroll for (int m = 0; m < WARP_TILE_M / WMMA_M; ++m) { wmma::fragment<wmma::matrix_a, WMMA_M, WMMA_N, WMMA_K, half, wmma::row_major> a_frag; int a_row = warp_m * WARP_TILE_M + m * WMMA_M; wmma::load_matrix_sync(a_frag, &sA[stage][a_row][0], BK + SKEW_A); wmma::mma_sync(acc[m][n], a_frag, b_frag, acc[m][n]); } } int next_k = k_tile + NumStages - 1; if (next_k < k_tiles) { int a_elems = BM * BK; int a_per_thread = a_elems / blockDim.x; int a_start = tid * a_per_thread; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < a_per_thread; i += 8) { int elem = a_start + i; int row = elem / BK; int col = elem % BK; const half* gmem = A + (block_m * BM + row) * K + (next_k * BK + col); half* smem = &sA[stage][row][col]; cp_async_16B(smem, gmem); } int b_elems = BN * BK; int b_per_thread = b_elems / blockDim.x; int b_start = tid * b_per_thread; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < b_per_thread; i += 8) { int elem = b_start + i; int col = elem / BK; int row = elem % BK; const half* gmem = B + (block_n * BN + col) * K + (next_k * BK + row); half* smem = &sB[stage][col][row]; cp_async_16B(smem, gmem); } cp_async_commit_group(); } stage = (stage + 1) % NumStages; } cp_async_wait_all(); __syncthreads(); // epilogue #pragma unroll for (int m = 0; m < WARP_TILE_M / WMMA_M; ++m) { for (int n = 0; n < WARP_TILE_N / WMMA_N; ++n) { int g_m = block_m * BM + warp_m * WARP_TILE_M + m * WMMA_M; int g_n = block_n * BN + warp_n * WARP_TILE_N + n * WMMA_N; if (beta != 0.0f && C != nullptr) { wmma::fragment<wmma::accumulator, WMMA_M, WMMA_N, WMMA_K, float> c_frag; wmma::load_matrix_sync(c_frag, C + g_m * N + g_n, N, wmma::mem_row_major); #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < acc[m][n].num_elements; ++i) { acc[m][n].x[i] = alpha * acc[m][n].x[i] + beta * c_frag.x[i]; } } else { #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < acc[m][n].num_elements; ++i) { acc[m][n].x[i] *= alpha; } } wmma::store_matrix_sync(D + g_m * N + g_n, acc[m][n], N, wmma::mem_row_major); } } } torch::Tensor gemm_forward( torch::Tensor A, torch::Tensor B, torch::Tensor C, float alpha, float beta) { int M = A.size(0); int K = A.size(1); int N = B.size(1); auto D = torch::empty({M, N}, torch::dtype(torch::kFloat32).device(A.device())); const half* A_ptr = reinterpret_cast<const half*>(A.data_ptr<at::Half>()); const half* B_ptr = reinterpret_cast<const half*>(B.data_ptr<at::Half>()); float* D_ptr = D.data_ptr<float>(); float* C_ptr = (C.numel() > 0) ? C.data_ptr<float>() : nullptr; dim3 grid(N / BN, M / BM); dim3 block(NUM_WARPS * warpSize); gemm_fp16_tensorcore_kernel<<<grid, block, 0, at::cuda::getCurrentCUDAStream()>>>( A_ptr, B_ptr, C_ptr, D_ptr, M, N, K, alpha, beta); return D; } """ ``` Wait, I used `WARP_TILE_M / WMMA_M` which is `64/16 = 4`. But `WARP_TILE_M` is not defined! I defined `WARPS_M` and `WARPS_N`, but not `WARP_TILE_M`. Let's define: ```cpp #define WARP_TILE_M (BM / WARPS_M) // 256/4 = 64 #define WARP_TILE_N (BN / WARPS_N) // 128/2 = 64 ``` Add these defines. Also need to ensure `warpSize` is known (32). **Potential issue**: In the prologue, if `k_tiles < NumStages - 1`, we might have fewer loads than stages. The main loop still runs `k_tiles` times. `cp_async_wait_group(NumStages - 2)` might wait for groups that were never committed. But `cp_async_wait_group` waits until the number of pending groups is <= N. If no groups were ever committed, it returns immediately. So it's safe. But there is a subtle bug: The prologue should also commit a dummy group? No. Actually, if `k_tiles == 0` (K=0), the kernel should produce 0 or not run. But K=4096, so irrelevant. **One more subtle bug**: In the prologue, we do `if (s < k_tiles)` and inside we commit. But `stage` increments regardless. This means that if `k_tiles` is small, some stages are skipped. The main loop uses `stage` starting from the value after prologue. The first `k_tile` might try to compute on a stage that was never loaded. But again, `k_tiles` is large. Still, to be robust, we can change the prologue to: ```cpp for (int s = 0; s < NumStages - 1 && s < k_tiles; ++s) { // load ... cp_async_commit_group(); stage = (stage + 1) % NumStages; } ``` This only iterates `min(NumStages-1, k_tiles)` times. Let's adjust. **Check the definition of `block_m` and `block_n`**: `block_m = blockIdx.y`, `block_n = blockIdx.x`. Grid: `dim3 grid(N / BN, M / BM);` -> `gridDim.x = N/BN`, `gridDim.y = M/BM`. So `blockIdx.x` iterates over N, `blockIdx.y` over M. Correct. **Check `wmma::load_matrix_sync` address calculation**: For A: `&sA[stage][a_row][0]`. `a_row` is multiple of 16. The sub-tile size is 16x16. This is fine. Stride: `BK + SKEW_A = 24` half elements. For B: `&sB[stage][b_col][0]`. `b_col` multiple of 16. Stride: `BK + SKEW_B = 24` half elements. **Check `cp_async_16B` alignment**: We need to ensure `gmem` is 16-byte aligned. For A, `A + (block_m*BM + row)*K + (k*BK + col)`. `col` is `elem % BK`. Since `elem` is multiple of 8, `col` is multiple of 8. K=4096, which is a multiple of 8. So the address offset `(block_m*BM + row)*K + (k*BK + col)` is a multiple of 8 half elements = 16 bytes. Because `K` is a multiple of 8, `row*K` is multiple of 16 bytes. `k*BK` is also multiple of 16 bytes (BK=16). `col` is multiple of 8. So total is multiple of 8 halfs = 16 bytes. Good. For B, `B + (block_n*BN + col)*K + (k*BK + row)`. `row` is `elem % BK`. `elem` is multiple of 8, so `row` is multiple of 8. Similarly, `col*K` is multiple of 16 bytes because K is multiple of 8. `k*BK` is multiple of 16. So address is multiple of 16 bytes. Good. Thus, alignment holds. **Potential issue with `cp_async` and row/col=0**: fine. **Potential issue with torch version and `warpSize`**: `warpSize` is a built-in variable in CUDA. In the host code, `warpSize` is not available. We used `NUM_WARPS * warpSize` in host code for `dim3 block(...)`. `warpSize` is not defined on host. We need to use `32` on the host side. ```cpp dim3 block(NUM_WARPS * 32); ``` In device code, `warpSize` is fine. **Splitting declarations**: We used `WARP_TILE_M / WMMA_M` in array dimensions. `WARP_TILE_M` is defined as `BM / WARPS_M`. Since `BM`, `WARPS_M` are compile-time constants, `WARP_TILE_M` is also a constant. This is fine. **Let's refine the compute loop**: Currently, for each `n`, we load `b_frag` once, then for each `m`, load `a_frag` and `mma_sync`. This is good. **Potential register pressure issue**: We have `acc[4][4]`, each fragment has 8 floats. So 4*4*8 = 128 floats = 512 bytes = 128 registers just for accumulators. Plus `a_frag` and `b_frag` per iteration (the compiler might keep them in registers). 128 registers per thread is a lot. With `__launch_bounds__(256, 3)`, max registers per thread is ~85. This will likely spill. Oh no. This is a critical issue. If accumulators alone need 128 registers, the kernel will spill. Spilling to local memory (which may be in L1 or global) will kill performance. Can we reduce the number of accumulators? Each warp computes a 64x64 tile. If we break it into smaller pieces and do multiple passes over K? No, the accumulators must be retained across all K iterations. We need to store the partial sums for the entire warp tile. But we can compute the warp tile in smaller chunks and write out incrementally? No, because alpha and beta scaling is done at the end. But we could accumulate into shared memory or do the epilogue in pieces. However, writing to shared memory or global memory multiple times also costs. Alternative: Only keep one accumulator fragment at a time and reuse it, writing to global memory after each sub-tile is complete. But the sub-tile is 16x16. If we process the warp tile as a sequence of 16x16 accumulations, each sub-tile would need to loop over all K iterations. That would mean reloading A and B fragments for each sub-tile. For a 64x64 tile, there are 16 sub-tiles. Reloading A and B from shared memory 16 times per K tile would be bad (16x more `ldmatrix` instructions). But maybe we can process the warp tile in 2x2 = 4 sub-tiles of 32x32? WMMA doesn't support 32x32. We can keep accumulators for a 32x32 region: that's 2x2 = 4 accumulators. 4*8 = 32 floats = 8 registers. Much better. But the warp computes 64x64. We can split it into 4 passes of 32x32. Each pass loops over all K tiles. However, this means reloading shared memory A and B for each pass. But we can keep the shared memory tile loaded for the whole K loop. The `ldmatrix` instructions would be issued 4 times more. That's okay if memory bandwidth is not the bottleneck. Wait, but the `ldmatrix` is per warp and reads from shared memory (fast). Increasing `ldmatrix` count by 4x might increase pressure but could still be okay. However, we also need to reload A fragments for each pass. The A fragment for a 32x32 sub-tile (2x2 WMMAs) requires 4 `ldmatrix` per K step. For 64x64, it's 16 `ldmatrix` per K step. So 4 passes = 4*4 = 16 `ldmatrix` total. Same total number! But the difference is register pressure: we keep only 4 accumulators (8 regs) per pass instead of 16 accumulators (32 regs). Actually, the compiler might keep `a_frag` and `b_frag` alive across iterations. But they can be reused. But there is a catch: We need to loop over K 4 times. That's 4x more `mma_sync`? No, total MMA count is the same: 64x64 tile = 16 WMMAs. Splitting into 4 passes of 4 WMMAs each, each pass loops over K tiles (256 iterations). So each pass issues 4*256 = 1024 MMAs. 4 passes = 4096 MMAs. In the original approach, we issue 16 MMAs per K tile * 256 = 4096 MMAs. Same total. The difference is that in the original, all 16 MMAs are in the inner loop, requiring 16 accumulator fragments live across the entire K loop. In the split approach, only 4 accumulators are live, but the K loop is executed 4 times. Is the split approach better for registers? Yes. What about performance? Re-executing the K loop 4 times means re-reading `sA` and `sB` from shared memory 4 times. Shared memory bandwidth on H100 is very high (maybe 10+ TB/s), but `ldmatrix` throughput might be limited. However, since K loop has many iterations and we have 24 warps per SM, re-reading shared memory might be okay. But it adds overhead: each pass needs `__syncthreads()`? No, because all warps in the block need to finish one K tile before moving to the next. If we split into passes, we still need to process all K tiles for the first pass, then all K tiles for the second pass, etc. That means between passes, we need to ensure all warps have finished. But they are in the same kernel. However, the block-level loop over K must be synchronized for shared memory (since we have a pipeline). The pipeline loads K tiles sequentially. If we loop over K 4 times, the pipeline will be reset each time. The prologue loads must happen again. That adds extra latency and global memory traffic (reading A and B again from global memory!). That's terrible! Because each pass would re-read A and B from global memory. We cannot do that. Alternative: Loop over M,N sub-tiles inside the warp, but keep K loop outermost. That is what we are doing. But we want fewer accumulators. We can process the warp tile as 2x2 sub-tiles inside the K loop: ```cpp for (int m = 0; m < 2; ++m) { for (int n = 0; n < 2; ++n) { // compute a 32x32 tile (2x2 WMMAs) over all K } } ``` But this would require an outer loop over the warp tile, with the K loop inside it. That means the K loop is executed 4 times, re-reading A and B global memory each time. Not good. What if we keep the K loop outermost but process a subset of the warp tile per K iteration, writing partial results to shared memory or registers? Not feasible. Better approach: Use fewer warps per block so each warp tile is larger? No, that doesn't reduce accumulators per warp. What if we use a warp tile of 32x64? Let's consider 16 warps (512 threads). Block tile 256x256. Warp tile 64x64. Still 16 accumulators. Register pressure high. What is the actual register usage of `wmma::fragment`? The compiler might not allocate a full 32 registers for 8 floats because the fragment is an abstraction and the data is stored in registers in a specific pattern, but the compiler manages it. However, `wmma::fragment` is essentially a struct with an array. The compiler has to allocate registers to hold those values across the K loop. So 8 floats = 8 registers per fragment. 16 fragments = 128 registers. Plus `a_frag`, `b_frag`, and loop indices. Total maybe 150+. One issue: In the `cp_async_16B` helper, we use `asm volatile` with `cp.async.cg.shared.global`. The fourth operand is `src_size`. For non-predicated copies, we can set src_size = 16. The PTX syntax requires the size immediate to match. Our inline asm uses `%2` and `%3` with constraints `n` (immediate) and `r` (register). Wait, for `cp.async`, the size must be an immediate. The `src_size` for the "ignore" version can also be immediate? Actually, the PTX syntax is: `cp.async.cg.shared.global [%dst], [%src], %size, %src_size;` Where `%size` is an immediate, and `%src_size` is an optional immediate or register for zero-fill. I think both can be immediate or the 4th can be a register. But in our case, we copy exactly 16 bytes and src_size = 16. To be safe, we can pass both as immediates using `n` constraint. But PTX inline asm requires all output and input operands to be separated. Let's use this: ```cpp __device__ __forceinline__ void cp_async_16B(void* smem_ptr, const void* gmem_ptr) { unsigned int smem_int = __cvta_generic_to_shared(smem_ptr); asm volatile("cp.async.cg.shared.global [%0], [%1], %2, %2;\n" :: "r"(smem_int), "l"(gmem_ptr), "n"(16)); } ``` This uses `%2` for both size and src_size, which is valid PTX? The syntax is `cp.async.cg.shared.global [dst], [src], size, src_size;`. Using the same operand for both is okay if both are 16. Actually, I recall the syntax: `cp.async.cg.shared.global [%0], [%1], %2, %3;`. But if `%2` and `%3` are both `n(16)`, we can just write `cp.async.cg.shared.global [%0], [%1], %2, %2;`. Is that allowed? I think so; it's using the same immediate for both. But to be extra safe, we can pass two separate immediates: ```cpp asm volatile("cp.async.cg.shared.global [%0], [%1], %2, %3;\n" :: "r"(smem_int), "l"(gmem_ptr), "n"(16), "n"(16)); ``` Yes, this is fine. **Another issue**: `__cvta_generic_to_shared` is a built-in available in device code. __device__ __forceinline__ void cp_async_16B(void* smem_ptr, const void* gmem_ptr) { unsigned int smem_int = __cvta_generic_to_shared(smem_ptr); asm volatile("cp.async.cg.shared.global [%0], [%1], %2, %3;\n" :: "r"(smem_int), "l"(gmem_ptr), "n"(16), "n"(16)); } __device__ __forceinline__ void cp_async_wait_all() { asm volatile("cp.async.wait_all;\n" ::); } __launch_bounds__(256, 4) __global__ void gemm_fp16_tensorcore_kernel( const half* __restrict__ A, const half* __restrict__ B, const float* __restrict__ C, float* __restrict__ D, int M, int N, int K, float alpha, float beta) { int block_m = blockIdx.y; int block_n = blockIdx.x; int warp_id = threadIdx.x / 32; int warp_m = warp_id / WARPS_N; int warp_n = warp_id % WARPS_N; __shared__ alignas(16) half sA[NumStages][BM][BK + SKEW_A]; __shared__ alignas(16) half sB[NumStages][BN][BK + SKEW_B]; wmma::fragment<wmma::accumulator, WMMA_M, WMMA_N, WMMA_K, float> acc[WARP_TILE_M / WMMA_M][WARP_TILE_N / WMMA_N]; #pragma unroll for (int m = 0; m < WARP_TILE_M / WMMA_M; ++m) for (int n = 0; n < WARP_TILE_N / WMMA_N; ++n) wmma::fill_fragment(acc[m][n], 0.0f); int k_tiles = K / BK; int stage = 0; int tid = threadIdx.x; // prologue: load first (NumStages-1) tiles #pragma unroll for (int s = 0; s < NumStages - 1 && s < k_tiles; int b_start = tid * b_per_thread; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < b_per_thread; i += 8) { int elem = b_start + i; int col = elem / BK; int row = elem % BK; const half* gmem = B + (block_n * BN + col) * K + (s * BK + row); half* smem = &sB[stage][col][row]; cp_async_16B(smem, gmem); } cp_async_commit_group(); stage = (stage + 1) % NumStages; } // main loop for (int k_tile = 0; k_tile < k_tiles; ++k_tile) { cp_async_wait_group(NumStages - 2); __syncthreads(); #pragma unroll for (int m = 0; m < WARP_TILE_M / WMMA_M; ++m) { wmma::fragment<wmma::matrix_a, WMMA_M, WMMA_N, WMMA_K, half, wmma::row_major> a_frag; int a_row = warp_m * WARP_TILE_M + m * WMMA_M; int b_start = tid * b_per_thread; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < b_per_thread; i += 8) { int elem = b_start + i; int col = elem / BK; int row = elem % BK; const half* gmem = B + (block_n * BN + col) * K + (next_k * BK + row); half* smem = &sB[stage][col][row]; cp_async_16B(smem, gmem); } cp_async_commit_group(); } stage = (stage + 1) % NumStages; } cp_async_wait_all(); __syncthreads(); // epilogue #pragma unroll for (int m = 0; m < WARP_TILE_M / WMMA_M; ++m) { for (int n = 0; n < WARP_TILE_N / WMMA_N; ++n) { int g_m = block_m * BM + warp_m * WARP_TILE_M + m * WMMA_M; int g_n = block_n * BN + warp_n * WARP_TILE_N + n * WMMA_N; } } } torch::Tensor gemm_forward( torch::Tensor A, torch::Tensor B, torch::Tensor C, float alpha, float beta) { int M = A.size(0); int K = A.size(1); int N = B.size(1); auto D = torch::empty({M, N}, torch::dtype(torch::kFloat32).device(A.device())); const half* A_ptr = reinterpret_cast<const half*>(A.data_ptr<at::Half>()); const half* B_ptr = reinterpret_cast<const half*>(B.data_ptr<at::Half>()); float* D_ptr = D.data_ptr<float>(); float* C_ptr = (C.numel() > 0) ? C.data_ptr<float>() : nullptr; dim3 grid(N / BN, M / BM); dim3 block(256); gemm_fp16_tensorcore_kernel<<<grid, block, 0, at::cuda::getCurrentCUDAStream()>>>( A_ptr, B_ptr, C_ptr, D_ptr, M, N, K, alpha, beta); return D; } """ class ModelNew(nn.Module): def __init__(self, K, N, alpha, beta): super().__init__() self.alpha = alpha self.beta = beta # Column-major weight: shape (K, N) with leading dimension K self.weight = nn.Parameter(torch.randn(N, K, dtype=torch.float16, device='cuda').t()) if beta != 0.0: self.C = nn.Parameter(torch.randn(M, N, dtype=torch.float32, device='cuda')) else: self.register_buffer('C', None) self.gemm = load_inline( name='gemm_hopper_fp16', cpp_sources='', cuda_sources=cuda_source, functions=['gemm_forward'], extra_cuda_cflags=['-O3', '--use_fast_math', '-gencode=arch=compute_90,code=sm_90'], verbose=False, ) def forward(self, A): A_half = A.contiguous().half() C_tensor = self.C if self.C is not None else torch.empty(0, dtype=torch.float32, device='cuda') return self.gemm.gemm_forward(A_half, self.weight, C_tensor, self.alpha, self.beta) ```

Let me write a complete, clean implementation: ```python import torch from torch.utils.cpp_extension import load_inline cuda_source = r""" #include <torch/extension.h> #include <cuda_fp16.h> #include <mma.h> #define BM 256 #define BN 128 #define BK 64 #define NumStages 4 #define SKEW 8 #define WARPS_M 4 #define WARPS_N 2 #define NUM_WARPS (WARPS_M * WARPS_N) #define WARP_TILE_M (BM / WARPS_M) #define WARP_TILE_N (BN / WARPS_N) using namespace nvcuda; // Helper for cp.async __device__ __forceinline__ void cp_async_16B(void* smem, const void* gmem) { unsigned smem_int = __cvta_generic_to_shared(smem); asm volatile("cp.async.cg.shared.global [%0], [%1], 16, 16;\n" :: "r"(smem_int), "l"(gmem)); } __device__ __forceinline__ void cp_async_commit() { asm volatile("cp.async.commit_group;\n" ::); } __device__ __forceinline__ void cp_async_wait_group(int n) { asm volatile("cp.async.wait_group %0;\n" :: "n"(n)); } __device__ __forceinline__ void cp_async_wait_all() { asm volatile("cp.async.wait_all;\n" ::); } __launch_bounds__(256, 1) __global__ void gemm_hopper_kernel( const half* __restrict__ A, const half* __restrict__ B, const float* __restrict__ C, float* __restrict__ D, int M, int N, int K, float alpha, float beta) { const int block_m = blockIdx.y; const int block_n = blockIdx.x; const int warp_id = threadIdx.x >> 5; const int warp_m = warp_id / WARPS_N; const int warp_n = warp_id % WARPS_N; const int tid = threadIdx.x; __shared__ alignas(16) half sA[NumStages][BM][BK + SKEW]; __shared__ alignas(16) half sB[NumStages][BN][BK + SKEW]; wmma::fragment<wmma::accumulator, 16, 16, 16, float> acc[WARP_TILE_M / 16][WARP_TILE_N / 16]; #pragma unroll for (int m = 0; m < WARP_TILE_M / 16; ++m) #pragma unroll for (int n = 0; n < WARP_TILE_N / 16; ++n) wmma::fill_fragment(acc[m][n], 0.0f); const int num_k_tiles = K / BK; int stage = 0; // Prologue: load first (NumStages - 1) stages #pragma unroll for (int s = 0; s < NumStages - 1 && s < num_k_tiles; ++s) { // Load A: BM x BK elements int a_total = BM * BK; int a_per_thread = (a_total + blockDim.x - 1) / blockDim.x; int a_begin = tid * a_per_thread; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < a_per_thread; i += 8) { int idx = a_begin + i; if (idx < a_total) { int row = idx / BK; int col = idx % BK; const half* gmem = A + (block_m * BM + row) * K + (s * BK + col); half* smem = &sA[stage][row][col]; cp_async_16B(smem, gmem); } } // Load B: BN x BK elements (B is ColumnMajor: B[k][n] at n*K + k) int b_total = BN * BK; int b_per_thread = (b_total + blockDim.x - 1) / blockDim.x; int b_begin = tid * b_per_thread; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < b_per_thread; i += 8) { int idx = b_begin + i; if (idx < b_total) { int n = idx / BK; int k = idx % BK; const half* gmem = B + (block_n * BN + n) * K + (s * BK + k); half* smem = &sB[stage][n][k]; cp_async_16B(smem, gmem); } } cp_async_commit(); stage = (stage + 1) % NumStages; } // Main loop for (int k_tile = 0; k_tile < num_k_tiles; ++k_tile) { cp_async_wait_group(NumStages - 2); __syncthreads(); // MMA computation #pragma unroll for (int kk = 0; kk < BK / 16; ++kk) { #pragma unroll for (int m = 0; m < WARP_TILE_M / 16; ++m) { wmma::fragment<wmma::matrix_a, 16, 16, 16, half, wmma::row_major> a_frag; int a_row = warp_m * WARP_TILE_M + m * 16; wmma::load_matrix_sync(a_frag, &sA[stage][a_row][kk * 16], BK + SKEW); #pragma unroll for (int n = 0; n < WARP_TILE_N / 16; ++n) { wmma::fragment<wmma::matrix_b, 16, 16, 16, half, wmma::col_major> b_frag; int b_n = warp_n * WARP_TILE_N + n * 16; wmma::load_matrix_sync(b_frag, &sB[stage][b_n][kk * 16], BK + SKEW); wmma::mma_sync(acc[m][n], a_frag, b_frag, acc[m][n]); } } } // Load next tile int next_k = k_tile + NumStages - 1; if (next_k < num_k_tiles) { int a_total = BM * BK; int a_per_thread = (a_total + blockDim.x - 1) / blockDim.x; int a_begin = tid * a_per_thread; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < a_per_thread; i += 8) { int idx = a_begin + i; if (idx < a_total) { int row = idx / BK; int col = idx % BK; const half* gmem = A + (block_m * BM + row) * K + (next_k * BK + col); half* smem = &sA[stage][row][col]; cp_async_16B(smem, gmem); } } int b_total = BN * BK; int b_per_thread = (b_total + blockDim.x - 1) / blockDim.x; int b_begin = tid * b_per_thread; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < b_per_thread; i += 8) { int idx = b_begin + i; if (idx < b_total) { int n = idx / BK; int k = idx % BK; const half* gmem = B + (block_n * BN + n) * K + (next_k * BK + k); half* smem = &sB[stage][n][k]; cp_async_16B(smem, gmem); } } cp_async_commit(); } stage = (stage + 1) % NumStages; } cp_async_wait_all(); __syncthreads(); // Epilogue #pragma unroll for (int m = 0; m < WARP_TILE_M / 16; ++m) { #pragma unroll for (int n = 0; n < WARP_TILE_N / 16; ++n) { int d_m = block_m * BM + warp_m * WARP_TILE_M + m * 16; int d_n = block_n * BN + warp_n * WARP_TILE_N + n * 16; if (beta != 0.0f && C != nullptr) { wmma::fragment<wmma::accumulator, 16, 16, 16, float> c_frag; wmma::load_matrix_sync(c_frag, C + d_m * N + d_n, N, wmma::mem_row_major); #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < acc[m][n].num_elements; ++i) { acc[m][n].x[i] = alpha * acc[m][n].x[i] + beta * c_frag.x[i]; } } else { #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < acc[m][n].num_elements; ++i) { acc[m][n].x[i] *= alpha; } } wmma::store_matrix_sync(D + d_m * N + d_n, acc[m][n], N, wmma::mem_row_major); } } } torch::Tensor gemm_forward(torch::Tensor A, torch::Tensor B, torch::Tensor C, float alpha, float beta) { int M = A.size(0); int K = A.size(1); int N = B.size(0); // B is (N, K) ColumnMajor, so size(0) is N auto D = torch::empty({M, N}, torch::dtype(torch::kFloat32).device(A.device())); const half* A_ptr = reinterpret_cast<const half*>(A.data_ptr<at::Half>()); const half* B_ptr = reinterpret_cast<const half*>(B.data_ptr<at::Half>()); float* D_ptr = D.data_ptr<float>(); float* C_ptr = (C.numel() > 0) ? C.data_ptr<float>() : nullptr; dim3 grid(N / BN, M / BM); dim3 block(256); size_t smem = 0; // static shared memory gemm_hopper_kernel<<<grid, block, smem, at::cuda::getCurrentCUDAStream()>>>( A_ptr, B_ptr, C_ptr, D_ptr, M, N, K, alpha, beta); return D; } """ class ModelNew(nn.Module): def __init__(self, K, N, alpha, beta): super().__init__() self.alpha = alpha self.beta = beta # Weight stored as (N, K) RowMajor, which maps to ColumnMajor (K, N) in linear memory self.weight = nn.Parameter(torch.randn(N, K, dtype=torch.float16, device='cuda')) if beta != 0.0: self.C = nn.Parameter(torch.randn(M, N, dtype=torch.float32, device='cuda')) else: self.register_buffer('C', torch.empty(0, dtype=torch.float32, device='cuda')) self.gemm = load_inline( name='gemm_hopper_fp16_v2', cpp_sources='', cuda_sources=cuda_source, functions=['gemm_forward'], extra_cuda_cflags=['-O3', '--use_fast_math', '-gencode=arch=compute_90,code=sm_90'], verbose=False, ) def forward(self, A): A_half = A.contiguous().half() return self.gemm.gemm_forward(A_half, self.weight, self.C, self.alpha, self.beta) ``` Wait, I need to be careful about `wmma::load_matrix_sync` with `mem_row_major`. __device__ __forceinline__ void cp_async_16B(void* smem_ptr, const void* gmem_ptr) { unsigned int smem_int = __cvta_generic_to_shared(smem_ptr); asm volatile("cp.async.cg.shared.global [%0], [%1], 16;\n" :: "r"(smem_int), "l"(gmem_ptr)); } __device__ __forceinline__ void cp_async_commit_group() { asm volatile("cp.async.commit_group;\n" ::); } __device__ __forceinline__ void cp_async_wait_group(int n) { asm volatile("cp.async.wait_group %0;\n" ::"n"(n)); } __device__ __forceinline__ void cp_async_wait_all() { asm volatile("cp.async.wait_all;\n" ::); } __launch_bounds__(THREADS_PER_BLOCK, 1) __global__ void gemm_hopper_kernel( const half* __restrict__ A, const half* __restrict__ B, const float* __restrict__ C, float* __restrict__ D, int M, int N, int K, float alpha, float beta) { const int block_m = blockIdx.y; __shared__ alignas(16) half sB[NumStages][BN][BK + SKEW]; wmma::fragment<wmma::accumulator, 16, 16, 16, float> acc[WARP_TILE_M / 16][WARP_TILE_N / 16]; #pragma unroll for (int m = 0; m < WARP_TILE_M / 16; ++m) #pragma unroll for (int n = 0; n < WARP_TILE_N / 16; ++n) wmma::fill_fragment(acc[m][n], 0.0f); const int num_k_tiles = K / BK; int load_stage = 0; // Prologue: load first (NumStages - 1) stages #pragma unroll for (int s = 0; wmma::mma_sync(acc[m][n], a_frag, b_frag, acc[m][n]); } } } // Load next tile int next_k = k_tile + NumStages - 1; if (next_k < num_k_tiles) { constexpr int a_total = BM * BK; constexpr int a_per_thread = a_total / THREADS_PER_BLOCK; int a_begin = tid * a_per_thread; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < a_per_thread; i += 8) { int idx = a_begin + i; int row = idx / BK; int col = idx % BK; const half* gmem = A + (block_m * BM + row) * K + (next_k * BK + col); } cp_async_commit_group(); load_stage = (load_stage + 1) % NumStages; } compute_stage = (compute_stage + 1) % NumStages; } cp_async_wait_all(); __syncthreads(); // Epilogue #pragma unroll for (int m = 0; m < WARP_TILE_M / 16; ++m) { #pragma unroll for (int n = 0; n < WARP_TILE_N / 16; ++n) { int d_m = block_m * BM + warp_m * WARP_TILE_M + m * 16; int d_n = block_n * BN + warp_n * WARP_TILE_N + n * 16; i < acc[m][n].num_elements; ++i) { acc[m][n].x[i] = alpha * acc[m][n].x[i] + beta * c_frag.x[i]; } } else { #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < acc[m][n].num_elements; ++i) { acc[m][n].x[i] *= alpha; } } wmma::store_matrix_sync(D + d_m * N + d_n, acc[m][n], N, wmma::mem_row_major); } } } torch::Tensor gemm_forward(torch::Tensor A, torch::Tensor B, torch::Tensor C, float alpha, float beta) { int M = A.size(0); int K = A.size(1); int N = B.size(0); auto D = torch::empty({M, N}, torch::dtype(torch::kFloat32).device(A.device())); const half* A_ptr = reinterpret_cast<const half*>(A.data_ptr<at::Half>()); const half* B_ptr = reinterpret_cast<const half*>(B.data_ptr<at::Half>()); float* D_ptr = D.data_ptr<float>(); float* C_ptr = (C.numel() > 0) ? C.data_ptr<float>() : nullptr; dim3 grid(N / BN, M / BM); dim3 block(THREADS_PER_BLOCK); gemm_hopper_kernel<<<grid, block, 0, at::cuda::getCurrentCUDAStream()>>>( A_ptr, B_ptr, C_ptr, D_ptr, M, N, K, alpha, beta); return D; } """ class ModelNew(nn.Module): def __init__(self, K, N, alpha, beta): super().__init__() self.alpha = alpha self.beta = beta # Store weight as (N, K) which when viewed linearly as n*K+k matches ColumnMajor (K,N) self.weight = nn.Parameter(torch.randn(N, K, dtype=torch.float16, device='cuda')) if beta != 0.0: self.C = nn.Parameter(torch.randn(M, N, dtype=torch.float32, device='cuda')) else: self.register_buffer('C', torch.empty(0, dtype=torch.float32, device='cuda')) self.gemm = load_inline( name='gemm_hopper_fp16_v3', cpp_sources='', cuda_sources=cuda_source, functions=['gemm_forward'], extra_cuda_cflags=['-O3', '--use_fast_math', '-gencode=arch=compute_90,code=sm_90'], verbose=False, ) def forward(self, A): A_half = A.contiguous().half() return self.gemm.gemm_forward(A_half, self.weight, self.C, self.alpha, self.beta) ``` Let me compile and correct this kernel

threadIdx.x;\n\n __shared__ alignas(16) half sA[NumStages][BM][BK + SKEW];\n __shared__ alignas(16) half sB[NumStages][BN][BK + SKEW];\n\n wmma::fragment<wmma::accumulator, 16, 16, 16, float> acc[WARP_TILE_M / 16][WARP_TILE_N / 16];\n #pragma unroll\n for (int m = 0; m < WARP_TILE_M / 16; ++m)\n #pragma unroll\n for (int n = 0; n < WARP_TILE_N / 16; ++n)\n wmma::fill_fragment(acc[m][n], gmem);\n }\n cp_async_commit_group();\n load_stage = (load_stage + 1) % NumStages;\n }\n\n int compute_stage = 0;\n\n // Main loop\n for (int k_tile = 0; k_tile < num_k_tiles; ++k_tile) {\n wmma::load_matrix_sync(b_frag, &sB[compute_stage][b_n][kk * 16], BK + SKEW);\n wmma::mma_sync(acc[m][n], a_frag, b_frag, acc[m][n]);\n }\n }\n }\n\n // Load next tile\n int next_k = k_tile + NumStages - 1;\n if (next_k < num_k_tiles) {\n constexpr int a_total = BM * BK;\n constexpr int a_per_thread = a_total / THREADS_PER_BLOCK;\n int a_begin = tid * a_per_thread;\n #pragma unroll\n for (int i = 0; i < a_per_thread; i += 8) {\n int idx = a_begin + i;\n int row = idx / BK;\n int col = idx % BK;\n const half* gmem = A + (block_m * BM + row) * K + (next_k * BK + col);\n half* smem = &sA[load_stage][row][col];\n cp_async_16B(smem, gmem);\n }\n\n constexpr int b_total = BN * BK;\n constexpr int b_per_thread = b_total / THREADS_PER_BLOCK;\n int b_begin = tid * b_per_thread;\n #pragma unroll\n for (int i = 0; i < b_per_thread; i += 8) {\n int idx = b_begin + i;\n int n = idx / BK;\n int k = idx % BK;\n const half* gmem = B + (block_n * BN + n) * K + (next_k * BK + k);\n half* smem = &sB[load_stage][n][k];\n cp_async_16B(smem, gmem);\n }\n cp_async_commit_group();\n load_stage = (load_stage + 1) % NumStages;\n }\n\n compute_stage = (compute_stage + 1) % NumStages;\n Actually, compile_kernel passed for v6 - so the syntax is good. But does it pass run_correctness? Let me run it. However, I also need to consider: the problem specifies that the hidden reference implementation likely computes `D = alpha * A * B + beta * C` where B is (K,N) ColumnMajor. In my kernel, I read B as: - `B + (block_n * BN + n) * K + (s * BK + k)` If B is ColumnMajor (K, N), then B[n, k] in col-major is at offset `k + n*K`. That is `n*K + k`. Wait, my indexing is `(block_n * BN + n) * K + (s * BK + k)`, which is `n*K + k`. This is correct for column major when `n` is the column index! Wait, B is sized [N][K] when I create it as `torch.randn(N, K)`. But ColumnMajor means elements are stored with column-major ordering: the first index is the row and second is the column, but contiguous memory traverses columns first. Actually in CUTLASS, `cutlass::layout::ColumnMajor` means matrix elements [m, n] are at offset `m + n*lda`. For a tensor of shape (K rows, N cols), element [k, n] is at offset `k + n*K`. In my kernel, `B + (block_n * BN + n) * K + (s * BK + k)` is exactly `n*K + k` if `block_n*BN + n` is the column index n. This matches column major! So my indexing is correct for column major. For A, `A + (block_m * BM + row) * K + (s * BK + col)` means element [row, col] is at `row*K + col`. This is row major, which is correct. So my memory access patterns are correct. Now let me run correctness. But wait - the compile_kernel tool compiles locally, not on the eval machine. Only run_correctness runs on the eval machine. Wait, looking at the tool outputs, compile_kernel can pass while run_correctness can still fail due to eval machine specifics (e.g. the shared memory limit error I got earlier was actually a compile-time error from run_correctness, meaning the eval machine was compiling it). But with launch_bounds of 2 blocks per SM, that would be 122KB, which is still below 228KB. So this should be fine. But I notice that H100 supports up to 227KB shared memory per thread block. C.data_ptr<float>() : nullptr;\n\n dim3 grid(N / BN, M / BM);\n dim3 block(THREADS_PER_BLOCK);\n\n gemm_hopper_kernel<<<grid, block>>>(\n A_ptr, B_ptr, C_ptr, D_ptr, M, N, K, alpha, beta);\n\n return D;\n}\n\"\"\"\n\nclass ModelNew(nn.Module):\n def __init__(self, K, N, alpha, beta):\n super().__init__()\n self.alpha = alpha\n self.beta = beta\n self.weight = nn.Parameter(torch.randn(N, K, dtype=torch.float16, device='cuda'))\n if beta != 0.0:\n self.C = nn.Parameter(torch.randn(M, N, dtype=torch.float32, device='cuda'))\n else:\n self.register_buffer('C', torch.empty(0, dtype=torch.float32, device='cuda'))\n\n self.gemm = load_inline(\n name='gemm_hopper_fp16_v6',\n cpp_sources=cpp_source,\n cuda_sources=cuda_source,\n functions=['gemm_forward'],\n extra_cuda_cflags=['-O3', '--use_fast_math', '-gencode=arch=compute_90,code=sm_90'],\n verbose=False,\n )\n\n def forward(self, A):\n A_half = A.contiguous().half()\n return self.gemm.gemm_forward(A_half, self.weight, self.C, self.alpha, self.beta)\n" }
